=== PROMPT ===
You will be given a competitive programming problem.
You are given an integer array nums and two integers, x and k. You can perform the following operation any number of times (including zero):

Increase or decrease any element of nums by 1.

Return the minimum number of operations needed to have at least k non-overlapping subarrays of size exactly x in nums, where all elements within each subarray are equal.
 
Example 1:

Input: nums = [5,-2,1,3,7,3,6,4,-1], x = 3, k = 2
Output: 8
Explanation:

Use 3 operations to add 3 to nums[1] and use 2 operations to subtract 2 from nums[3]. The resulting array is [5, 1, 1, 1, 7, 3, 6, 4, -1].
Use 1 operation to add 1 to nums[5] and use 2 operations to subtract 2 from nums[6]. The resulting array is [5, 1, 1, 1, 7, 4, 4, 4, -1].
Now, all elements within each subarray [1, 1, 1] (from indices 1 to 3) and [4, 4, 4] (from indices 5 to 7) are equal. Since 8 total operations were used, 8 is the output.


Example 2:

Input: nums = [9,-2,-2,-2,1,5], x = 2, k = 2
Output: 3
Explanation:

Use 3 operations to subtract 3 from nums[4]. The resulting array is [9, -2, -2, -2, -2, 5].
Now, all elements within each subarray [-2, -2] (from indices 1 to 2) and [-2, -2] (from indices 3 to 4) are equal. Since 3 operations were used, 3 is the output.


 
Constraints:

2 <= nums.length <= 10^5
-10^6 <= nums[i] <= 10^6
2 <= x <= nums.length
1 <= k <= 15
2 <= k * x <= nums.length

Use the following starter code to write the final solution.
```python
class Solution:
    def minOperations(self, nums: List[int], x: int, k: int) -> int:
        
```

Reason in a commit-implement-recheck way.

A productive rhythm for this task usually looks like this:
- Isolate the decisive constraints, the target complexity, and at most one or two approaches that are still truly plausible.
- If two candidates remain, compare them only on the decisive constraint and commit quickly to the stronger one instead of keeping a wide search open.
- Before coding, keep only a tiny implementation ledger for the chosen plan: main invariant, key state or transition, and the one or two edge cases most likely to fail.
- Move into code as soon as that ledger is clear. After coding starts, keep reasoning attached to the current draft rather than reopening the high-level search.
- After the first complete draft, do one short implementation-check-adjust-check loop on the same draft: test the key edge case, patch locally if needed, and recheck.
- Reopen the main design only if a concrete counterexample breaks the core invariant. Otherwise prefer the local patch and keep closing.
- Once the current draft, invariant, and short recheck still agree, finalize the solution.

Try to avoid patterns like these:
- spending too long comparing nearby plans before committing to one workable approach;
- returning from code to broad algorithm discussion without a concrete contradiction;
- turning one local doubt into a full restart when the current draft only needs a small patch.

Output only the final Python code enclosed in a code block.

Coordinate compression:
vals = sorted(set(nums))
m = len(vals)
bit_count = [0]*(m+1)
bit_sum = [0]*(m+1)

def add(idx, delta):
    # idx 1-based
    while idx <= m:
        bit_count[idx] += delta
        bit_sum[idx] += delta * val? Wait update by value: need actual value for sum. We can pass val. If adding/removing nums[pos], update bit_sum with delta*val. We can use vals[idx-1].
        idx += idx & -idx

def query_count(idx):
    s=0
    while idx>0: s += bit_count[idx]; idx -= idx&-idx
    return s

def query_sum(idx):
    s=0
    while idx>0: s += bit_sum[idx]; idx -= idx&-idx
    return s

def find_kth(k):
    # smallest idx such that prefix_count >= k
    idx=0
    bitmask=1 << (m.bit_length()-1) maybe highest power of two <= m? Standard.
    while bitmask:
        nxt = idx + bitmask
        if nxt <= m and bit_count[nxt] < k:
            idx = nxt
            k -= bit_count[nxt]
        bitmask >>=1
    return idx + 1

Now, need ensure DP with rolling rows:
costs length n-x+1.
n = len(nums)
dp_prev = [0]*(n+1)  # j=0
for j in range(1, k+1):
    dp_cur = [INF]*(n+1)
    # dp_cur[0] remains INF
    for i in range(1, n+1):
        best = dp_cur[i-1]

            base = dp_prev[start]
            if base + costs[start] < best:
                best = base + costs[start]
        dp_cur[i] = best
    dp_prev = dp_cur
return dp_prev[n]

bitmask = 1 << (m.bit_length() - 1)
while bitmask:
    nxt = idx + bitmask
    if nxt <= m and bit_count[nxt] < k:
        idx = nxt
        k -= bit_count[nxt]
    bitmask >>= 1
return idx + 1
This returns smallest index with cumulative count >= original k. Need if m=0? n>=2 so m>=1. Good.

Now, let's verify DP recurrence with prefix length and cost indexing thoroughly.

Let cost[s] = cost to make interval [s, s+x-1] uniform.
Define dp[j][i] = min cost to choose j non-overlapping intervals entirely within first i elements (indices 0..i-1). For j=0, dp[0][i]=0.
For j>=1:
- If last chosen interval ends at i-1? It starts at i-x. Previous j-1 intervals must be entirely within indices 0..i-x-1, i.e., prefix length i-x. Cost = dp[j-1][i-x] + cost[i-x].
- Or no interval ends at i-1, so dp[j][i-1].
Thus recurrence. Need also if i < x, no interval can end at i-1, so dp[j][i]=dp[j][i-1]. For i=x, start0, previous prefix length0, dp[j-1][0]. For j=1, dp0[0]=0. For j>1, dp[j-1][0]=INF (since cannot choose >0 intervals in empty prefix). Good. Our rolling prev[start] for j>1 at start=0 is INF? Let's see after computing dp1, dp1[0]=INF? But wait for j=2, first interval could start0, second interval start x. For i=2x, start=x, prev[x]=dp1[x] = cost0 (one interval in prefix length x). Candidate cost0+cost[x]. Good. For i=x, cannot choose 2 intervals in length x, INF. Good.

But for j=1, prev=dp0, prev[0]=0. Good.

Now, what about dp[j][i] for i less than j*x? It should remain INF. Recurrence with dp_cur[i-1] INF and candidates only if i>=x, but prev[start] maybe INF for j-1 not enough. Good.

from typing import List

class Solution:
    def minOperations(self, nums: List[int], x: int, k: int) -> int:
        n = len(nums)
        vals = sorted(set(nums))
        m = len(vals)
        # map value to 1-based index
        # Since vals sorted, use dict for O(1)
        comp = {v: i+1 for i, v in enumerate(vals)}
        bit_cnt = [0]*(m+1)
        bit_sum = [0]*(m+1)
        freq = [0]*(m+1)
        total_sum = 0

        def add(idx, delta, val):
            nonlocal? If nested function modifies bit arrays not rebind, no nonlocal needed. total_sum not modified inside? We'll update total_sum outside. But add uses bit arrays. 
            while idx <= m:
                bit_cnt[idx] += delta
                bit_sum[idx] += delta * val
                idx += idx & -idx
            freq[idx]? Wait if we modify idx in loop, cannot use idx after. Need store original idx. Use orig = idx. 
        def add(idx, delta, val):
            freq[idx] += delta
            while idx <= m:
                bit_cnt[idx] += delta
                bit_sum[idx] += delta * val
                idx += idx & -idx

        def prefix_count(idx):
            s=0
            while idx>0:
                s += bit_cnt[idx]
                idx -= idx & -idx
            return s

        def prefix_sum(idx):
            s=0
            while idx>0:
                s += bit_sum[idx]
                idx -= idx & -idx
            return s

        def kth(k):
            idx=0
            bit = 1 << (m.bit_length() - 1)
            while bit:
                nxt = idx + bit
                if nxt <= m and bit_cnt[nxt] < k:
                    idx = nxt
                    k -= bit_cnt[nxt]
                bit >>= 1
            return idx + 1

        costs = [0]*(n - x + 1)
        # initialize first window
        for p in range(x):
            val = nums[p]
            idx = comp[val]
            add(idx, 1, val)
            total_sum += val
        # But add doesn't update total_sum; we do outside. Need total_sum variable in outer scope. If assigning total_sum inside loop in method, okay. But if inside for loop, no nested function. Good.
        for start in range(n - x + 1):
            med_idx = kth((x + 1)//2)
            med_val = vals[med_idx - 1]
            cnt_less = prefix_count(med_idx - 1)
            sum_less = prefix_sum(med_idx - 1)
            cnt_eq = freq[med_idx]
            sum_eq = med_val * cnt_eq
            cnt_greater = x - cnt_less - cnt_eq
            sum_greater = total_sum - sum_less - sum_eq
            costs[start] = med_val * cnt_less - sum_less + sum_greater - med_val * cnt_greater
            if start + x < n:
                out_val = nums[start]
                out_idx = comp[out_val]
                add(out_idx, -1, out_val)
                total_sum -= out_val
                in_val = nums[start + x]
                in_idx = comp[in_val]
                add(in_idx, 1, in_val)
                total_sum += in_val

Potential issue: In add function, if we use `freq[idx] += delta` then while modifies idx, but we need original for freq. Use orig = idx. Good.

But nested function `add` uses `m` from outer. Fine.

Could avoid function call overhead for add by inlining? Not necessary maybe. But for performance, we can define add as local and call 2n times. Function call overhead 2e5, okay. prefix_count/sum called 2 per window =2e5, kth 1e5. Fine.

Need ensure `total_sum` variable in loop: We assign total_sum += val inside initialization and sliding. Since not inside nested function, no nonlocal. Good.

Now, let's maybe implement DP with local variables for speed:
costs = ...
INF = 10**30
prev = [0]*(n+1)
for _ in range(k):
    cur = [INF]*(n+1)
    # local x, costs, prev
    for i in range(1, n+1):
        best = cur[i-1]
        if i >= x:
            start = i - x
            cand = prev[start] + costs[start]
            if cand < best:
                best = cand
        cur[i] = best
    prev = cur
return prev[n]

Need ensure for j=1, prev[start] =0 for all start. Yes. For j>1, prev[start] may be INF. Good.

Wait, for j=1, prev = [0]*(n+1). But dp0[i] should be 0 for all i, including i=0. Yes. So prev[start]=0. Good.

Now, let's think about if `cur[i-1]` for i=1 is `cur[0]` = INF. Good. For j=1 and x=2, at i=2, best=cur[1]=INF, candidate prev[0]+cost0=0+cost0. Good.

Now, let's consider if there is a need to use `at least k` and DP exactly k. We argued equal. But let's double-check with nonnegative costs and subset property: If a solution has m>k intervals, choose any k of them. Are they non-overlapping? Yes, subset of non-overlapping intervals. Cost to make those k intervals equal in original array? The original solution may have changed elements in all m intervals; but for the chosen k intervals, the final values in those intervals are equal. The cost for those elements is at most total cost? Actually if an element belongs to one of chosen intervals, operations on it counted. If an element belongs to unchosen interval, not counted. Now, let's think about possible issue with `find_kth` using `bit_cnt[nxt] < k` where `bit_cnt` is Fenwick tree, not frequency array. Standard algorithm uses tree array. Yes, bit_cnt[nxt] stores sum over range (nxt - lowbit +1 .. nxt). The algorithm:
idx=0
for bitmask from highest power of two <= n:
    t = idx + bitmask
    if t <= n and tree[t] < k:
        idx = t
        k -= tree[t]
return idx+1
This works because tree[t] is cumulative for that block. Good.

Let's write code with clear variable names:
from typing import List

class Solution:
    def minOperations(self, nums: List[int], x: int, k: int) -> int:
        n = len(nums)
        vals = sorted(set(nums))
        m = len(vals)
        comp = {v: i+1 for i, v in enumerate(vals)}
        bit_cnt = [0]*(m+1)
        bit_sum = [0]*(m+1)
        freq = [0]*(m+1)

        def add(idx: int, delta: int, val: int) -> None:
            freq[idx] += delta
            while idx <= m:
                bit_cnt[idx] += delta
                bit_sum[idx] += delta * val
                idx += idx & -idx

        def prefix_count(idx: int) -> int:
            s = 0
            while idx > 0:
                s += bit_cnt[idx]
                idx -= idx & -idx
            return s

        def prefix_sum(idx: int) -> int:
            s = 0
            while idx > 0:
                s += bit_sum[idx]
                idx -= idx & -idx
            return s

        def find_kth(order: int) -> int:
            idx = 0
            step = 1 << (m.bit_length() - 1)
            while step:
                nxt = idx + step
                if nxt <= m and bit_cnt[nxt] < order:
                    idx = nxt
                    order -= bit_cnt[nxt]
                step >>= 1
            return idx + 1

        total_sum = 0
        for p in range(x):
            val = nums[p]
            idx = comp[val]
            add(idx, 1, val)
            total_sum += val

        costs = [0]*(n - x + 1)
        lower_order = (x + 1)//2
        for start in range(n - x + 1):
            med_idx = find_kth(lower_order)
            med_val = vals[med_idx - 1]
            cnt_less = prefix_count(med_idx - 1)
            sum_less = prefix_sum(med_idx - 1)
            cnt_eq = freq[med_idx]
            sum_eq = med_val * cnt_eq
            cnt_greater = x - cnt_less - cnt_eq
            sum_greater = total_sum - sum_less - sum_eq
            costs[start] = med_val * cnt_less - sum_less + sum_greater - med_val * cnt_greater

            if start + x < n:
                out_val = nums[start]
                out_idx = comp[out_val]
                add(out_idx, -1, out_val)
                total_sum -= out_val

                in_val = nums[start + x]
                in_idx = comp[in_val]
                add(in_idx, 1, in_val)
                total_sum += in_val

        INF = 10**30
        prev = [0]*(n+1)
        for j in range(1, k+1):
            cur = [INF]*(n+1)
            # local references for speed
            prev_local = prev
            costs_local = costs
            for i in range(j * x, n + 1):
                best = cur[i-1]
                start = i - x
                cand = prev_local[start] + costs_local[start]
                if cand < best:
                    best = cand
                cur[i] = best
            prev = cur
        return prev[n]

Need check `prev_local = prev` inside loop; after `prev = cur`, next iteration uses new. Good. But `prev_local` variable not necessary. Could use `prev` directly. Fine.

Potential issue: In DP loop, `cur[i-1]` for i=j*x is INF because cur initialized INF and we haven't set earlier. But what if j*x -1 >= x and we set cur[j*x -1] in a previous iteration? No, this is a new cur list for current j, and we only loop from j*x. So no. Good.

Potential issue: For j=1, `range(x, n+1)`. For i=x, `best=cur[x-1]` INF. Good. But what about `cur[i-1]` for i=x+1 is cur[x] set. Good.
Potential issue: For j=2, `range(2*x, n+1)`. For i=2x, `best=cur[2x-1]` INF. Good. For i=2x+1, best=cur[2x] finite. Good.

For j=2, i=2x, start=x, prev[x]=dp1[x]=cost0. Good. For i=2x+1, start=x+1, prev[x+1]=dp1[x+1]=min(cost0,cost1). Good.

Now, let's consider if there is any issue with "at least k" and DP exactly k if there are zero-cost intervals and subset property. We already reasoned. But let's formalize: Let OPT_exact = min cost for exactly k. Since exactly k is a feasible solution for at least k, OPT_atleast <= OPT_exact. For any feasible solution with m>=k intervals and cost C, choose any k of its intervals; the cost to make those k intervals equal is at most C (because operations on their elements are subset). Thus there is an exactly k solution with cost <= C. Taking inf gives OPT_exact <= OPT_atleast. So equal. Good.

Now, let's think about if choosing any k intervals from m might require changing elements in those intervals to their final values, but if an element in chosen interval was also changed in original solution due to being in an unchosen interval? Intervals are non-overlapping, so no element belongs to two intervals. Thus operations on chosen interval elements are disjoint from unchosen. Good. If intervals are non-overlapping, yes. So subset cost exactly sum over chosen intervals' element changes, <= total. Good.

Now, let's maybe think about if there is a possible bug in DP due to using `prev` row from previous j but `prev[start]` for start = i-x where i is prefix length. Let's rederive with prefix length carefully one more time.

Let dp[j][i] = min cost for j intervals within nums[0:i] (i elements). For j=0, 0.
For j>=1, consider whether there is an interval ending at i-1. If yes, its start is i-x. It occupies [i-x, i-1]. Previous j-1 intervals must be within [0, i-x-1], which has length i-x. So cost = dp[j-1][i-x] + cost[i-x]. If no interval ending at i-1, cost = dp[j][i-1]. Thus recurrence. In code, for i from j*x to n, we compute min. For i < j*x, impossible. For i=j*x, dp[j][i-1] impossible, candidate dp[j-1][(j-1)x]+cost[(j-1)x]. Good.
Now, if there is an interval ending at i-1, it must start at i-x. But what if the last interval ends before i-1 and there is a gap at the end? Then no interval ending at i-1, covered by dp[j][i-1]. Good.
Thus DP correct.

Now, let's maybe think about if there is a possible bug in cost computation due to using lower median but `cnt_less` and `cnt_greater` relative to lower median. For even x, lower median m, values between m and upper median maybe include values equal to m? Actually if lower median m and upper median u, any t in [m,u] minimizes. Our formula with m: values less than m contribute m-a; values greater than m contribute a-m. If there are values equal to m, contribute0. If there are values between m and u, they are greater than m, contribute a-m. If we chose t=m, cost includes a-m for those. If chose t>m, cost for those decreases, but cost for values less than m increases. Sum constant until u. Our formula with m gives correct min. Good.
For x=2 [0,10], m=0, greater10 contributes10, total10. For t=5, cost5+5=10. Good.
For [0,5,10,15], x=4, lower_order=2 -> m=5. Values less:0 cost5; greater:10 cost5,15 cost10; total20. For t=10, cost10+5+0+5=20. Good.

Now, let's consider if there is any issue with `cnt_eq = freq[med_idx]` if there are multiple values equal to med, and some of them are "greater" in sorted order? Equal values indistinguishable; cost0. Good.

Now, let's maybe think about if there is a possible bug in cost computation due to using lower median but `cnt_less` and `cnt_greater` relative to lower median. For even x, lower median m, values between m and upper median maybe include values equal to m? Actually if lower median m and upper median u, any t in [m,u] minimizes. Our formula with m: values less than m contribute m-a; values greater than m contribute a-m. If there are values equal to m, contribute0. If there are values between m and u, they are greater than m, contribute a-m. If we chose t=m, cost includes a-m for those. If chose t>m, cost for those decreases, but cost for values less than m increases. Sum constant until u. Our formula with m gives correct min. Good.
For x=2 [0,10], m=0, greater10 contributes10, total10. For t=5, cost5+5=10. Good.
For [0,5,10,15], x=4, lower_order=2 -> m=5. Values less:0 cost5; greater:10 cost5,15 cost10; total20. For t=10, cost10+5+0+5=20. Good.

Our formula with m: values less than m contribute m-a; values greater than m contribute a-m. If there are values equal to m, contribute0. If there are values between m and u, they are greater than m, contribute a-m. If we chose t=m, cost includes a-m for those. If chose t>m, cost for those decreases, but cost for values less than m increases. Sum constant until u. Our formula with m gives correct min. Good.

Now, let's maybe think about if there is a possible bug in cost computation due to using lower median but `cnt_less` and `cnt_greater` relative to lower median. For even x, lower median m, values between m and upper median maybe include values equal to m? Actually if lower median m and upper median u, any t in [m,u] minimizes. Our formula with m: values less than m contribute m-a; values greater than m contribute a-m. If there are values equal to m, contribute0. If there are values between m and u, they are greater than m, contribute a-m. If we chose t=m, cost includes a-m for those. If chose t>m, cost for those decreases, but cost for values less than m increases. Sum constant until u. Our formula with m gives correct min. Good.
For x=2 [0,10], m=0, greater10 contributes10, total10. For t=5, cost5+5=10. Good.
For [0,5,10,15], x=4, lower_order=2 -> m=5. Values less:0 cost5; greater:10 cost5,15 cost10; total20. For t=10, cost10+5+0+5=20. Good.

For even x, lower median m, values between m and upper median maybe include values equal to m? Actually if lower median m and upper median u, any t in [m,u] minimizes. Our formula with m: values less than m contribute m-a; values greater than m contribute a-m. If there are values equal to m, contribute0. If there are values between m and u, they are greater than m, contribute a-m. If we chose t=m, cost includes a-m for those. If chose t>m, cost for those decreases, but cost for values less than m increases. Sum constant until u. Our formula with m gives correct min. Good.
For x=2 [0,10], m=0, greater10 contributes10, total10. For t=5, cost5+5=10. Good.
For [0,5,10,15], x=4, lower_order=2 -> m=5. Values less:0 cost5; greater:10 cost5,15 cost10; total20. For t=10, cost10+5+0+5=20. Good.

Now, let's maybe think about if there is a possible bug in cost computation due to using lower median but `cnt_less` and `cnt_greater` relative to lower median. For even x, lower median m, values between m and upper median maybe include values equal to m? Actually if lower median m and upper median u, any t in [m,u] minimizes. Our formula with m: values less than m contribute m-a; values greater than m contribute a-m. If there are values equal to m, contribute0. If there are values between m and u, they are greater than m, contribute a-m. If we chose t=m, cost includes a-m for those. If chose t>m, cost for those decreases, but cost for values less than m increases. Sum constant until u. Our formula with m gives correct min. Good.
For x=2 [0,10], m=0, greater10 contributes10, total10. For t=5, cost5+5=10. Good.
For [0,5,10,15], x=4, lower_order=2 -> m=5. Values less:0 cost5; greater:10 cost5,15 cost10; total20. For t=10, cost10+5+0+5=20. Good.

Now, let's maybe think about if there is a possible bug in cost computation due to using lower median but `cnt_less` and `cnt_greater` relative to lower median. For even x, lower median m, values between m and upper median maybe include values equal to m? Actually if lower median m and upper median u, any t in [m,u] minimizes. Our formula with m: values less than m contribute m-a; values greater than m contribute a-m. If there are values equal to m, contribute0. If there are values between m and u, they are greater than m, contribute a-m. If we chose t=m, cost includes a-m for those. If chose t>m, cost for those decreases, but cost for values less than m increases. Sum constant until u. Our formula with m gives correct min. Good.
For x=2 [0,10], m=0, greater10 contributes10, total10. For t=5, cost5+5=10. Good.
For [0,5,10,15], x=4, lower_order=2 -> m=5. Values less:0 cost5; greater:10 cost5,15 cost10; total20. For t=10, cost10+5+0+5=20. Good.

Now, let's maybe think about if there is a possible bug in cost computation due to using lower median but `cnt_less` and `cnt_greater` relative to lower median. For even x, lower median m, values between m and upper median maybe include values equal to m? Actually if lower median m and upper median u, any t in [m,u] minimizes. Our formula with m: values less than m contribute m-a; values greater than m contribute a-m. If there are values equal to m, contribute0. If there are values between m and u, they are greater than m, contribute a-m. If we chose t=m, cost includes a-m for those. If chose t>m, cost for those decreases, but cost for values less than m increases. Sum constant until u. Our formula with m gives correct min. Good.
For x=2 [0,10], m=0, greater10 contributes10, total10. For t=5, cost5+5=10. Good.
For [0,5,10,15], x=4, lower_order=2 -> m=5. Values less:0 cost5; greater:10 cost5,15 cost10; total20. For t=10, cost10+5+0+5=20. Good.

Now, let's maybe think about if there is a possible bug in cost computation due to using lower median but `cnt_less` and `cnt_greater` relative to lower median. For even x, lower median m, values between m and upper median maybe include values equal to m? Actually if lower median m and upper median u, any t in [m,u] minimizes. Our formula with m: values less than m contribute m-a; values greater than m contribute a-m. If there are values equal to m, contribute0. If there are values between m and u, they are greater than m, contribute a-m. If we chose t=m, cost includes a-m for those. If chose t>m, cost for those decreases, but cost for values less than m increases. Sum constant until u. Our formula with m gives correct min. Good.
For x=2 [0,10], m=0, greater10 contributes10, total10. For t=5, cost5+5=10. Good.
For [0,5,10,15], x=4, lower_order=2 -> m=5. Values less:0 cost5; greater:10 cost5,15 cost10; total20. For t=10, cost10+5+0+5=20. Good.

Now, let's maybe think about if there is a possible bug in cost computation due to using lower median but `cnt_less` and `cnt_greater` relative to lower median. For even x, lower median m, values between m and upper median maybe include values equal to m? Actually if lower median m and upper median u, any t in [m,u] minimizes. Our formula with m: values less than m contribute m-a; values greater than m contribute a-m. If there are values equal to m, contribute0. If there are values between m and u, they are greater than m, contribute a-m. If we chose t=m, cost includes a-m for those. If chose t>m, cost for those decreases, but cost for values less than m increases. Sum constant until u. Our formula with m gives correct min. Good.
For x=2 [0,10], m=0, greater10 contributes10, total10. For t=5, cost5+5=10. Good.
For [0,5,10,15], x=4, lower_order=2 -> m=5. Values less:0 cost5; greater:10 cost5,15 cost10; total20. For t=10, cost10+5+0+5=20. Good.

Now, let's maybe think about if there is a possible bug in cost computation due to using lower median but `cnt_less` and `cnt_greater` relative to lower median. For even x, lower median m, values between m and upper median maybe include values equal to m? Actually if lower median m and upper median u, any t in [m,u] minimizes. Our formula with m: values less than m contribute m-a; values greater than m contribute a-m. If there are values equal to m, contribute0. If there are values between m and u, they are greater than m, contribute a-m. If we chose t=m, cost includes a-m for those. If chose t>m, cost for those decreases, but cost for values less than m increases. Sum constant until u. Our formula with m gives correct min. Good.
For x=2 [0,10], m=0, greater10 contributes10, total10. For t=5, cost5+5=10. Good.
For [0,5,10,15], x=4, lower_order=2 -> m=5. Values less:0 cost5; greater:10 cost5,15 cost10; total20. For t=10, cost10+5+0+5=20. Good.

Now, let's maybe think about if there is a possible bug in cost computation due to using lower median but `cnt_less` and `cnt_greater` relative to lower median. For even x, lower median m, values between m and upper median maybe include values equal to m? Actually if lower median m and upper median u, any t in [m,u] minimizes. Our formula with m: values less than m contribute m-a; values greater than m contribute a-m. If there are values equal to m, contribute0. If there are values between m and u, they are greater than m, contribute a-m. If we chose t=m, cost includes a-m for those. If chose t>m, cost for those decreases, but cost for values less than m increases. Sum constant until u. Our formula with m gives correct min. Good.
For x=2 [0,10], m=0, greater10 contributes10, total10. For t=5, cost5+5=10. Good.
For [0,5,10,15], x=4, lower_order=2 -> m=5. Values less:0 cost5; greater:10 cost5,15 cost10; total20. For t=10, cost10+5+0+5=20. Good.

Now, let's maybe think about if there is a possible bug in cost computation due to using lower median but `cnt_less` and `cnt_greater` relative to lower median. For even x, lower median m, values between m and upper median maybe include values equal to m? Actually if lower median m and upper median u, any t in [m,u] minimizes. Our formula with m: values less than m contribute m-a; values greater than m contribute a-m. If there are values equal to m, contribute0. If there are values between m and u, they are greater than m, contribute a-m. If we chose t=m, cost includes a-m for those. If chose t>m, cost for those decreases, but cost for values less than m increases. Sum constant until u. Our formula with m gives correct min. Good.
For x=2 [0,10], m=0, greater10 contributes10, total10. For t=5, cost5+5=10. Good.
For [0,5,10,15], x=4, lower_order=2 -> m=5. Values less:0 cost5; greater:10 cost5,15 cost10; total20. For t=10, cost10+5+0+5=20. Good.

Now, let's consider if there is any issue with `sum_greater - med_val * cnt_greater` if med_val negative; e.g., med=-5, greater values maybe -1, sum

=== ANSWER ===
```python
from typing import List

class Solution:
    def minOperations(self, nums: List[int], x: int, k: int) -> int:
        n = len(nums)

        vals = sorted(set(nums))
        m = len(vals)
        comp = {v: i + 1 for i, v in enumerate(vals)}

        bit_cnt = [0] * (m + 1)
        bit_sum = [0] * (m + 1)
        freq = [0] * (m + 1)

        def add(idx: int, delta: int, val: int) -> None:
            freq[idx] += delta
            while idx <= m:
                bit_cnt[idx] += delta
                bit_sum[idx] += delta * val
                idx += idx & -idx

        def prefix_count(idx: int) -> int:
            s = 0
            while idx > 0:
                s += bit_cnt[idx]
                idx -= idx & -idx
            return s

        def prefix_sum(idx: int) -> int:
            s = 0
            while idx > 0:
                s += bit_sum[idx]
                idx -= idx & -idx
            return s

        top_bit = 1 << (m.bit_length() - 1)

        def find_kth(order: int) -> int:
            idx = 0
            step = top_bit
            while step:
                nxt = idx + step
                if nxt <= m and bit_cnt[nxt] < order:
                    idx = nxt
                    order -= bit_cnt[nxt]
                step >>= 1
            return idx + 1

        total_sum = 0
        for i in range(x):
            val = nums[i]
            idx = comp[val]
            add(idx, 1, val)
            total_sum += val

        costs = [0] * (n - x + 1)
        lower_order = (x + 1) // 2

        for start in range(n - x + 1):
            med_idx = find_kth(lower_order)
            med_val = vals[med_idx - 1]

            cnt_less = prefix_count(med_idx - 1)
            sum_less = prefix_sum(med_idx - 1)

            cnt_eq = freq[med_idx]
            sum_eq = med_val * cnt_eq

            cnt_greater = x - cnt_less - cnt_eq
            sum_greater = total_sum - sum_less - sum_eq

            costs[start] = (
                med_val * cnt_less - sum_less
                + sum_greater - med_val * cnt_greater
            )

            if start + x < n:
                out_val = nums[start]
                out_idx = comp[out_val]
                add(out_idx, -1, out_val)
                total_sum -= out_val

                in_val = nums[start + x]
                in_idx = comp[in_val]
                add(in_idx, 1, in_val)
                total_sum += in_val

        INF = 10 ** 30
        prev = [0] * (n + 1)

        for j in range(1, k + 1):
            cur = [INF] * (n + 1)
            for i in range(j * x, n + 1):
                best = cur[i - 1]
                start = i - x
                cand = prev[start] + costs[start]
                if cand < best:
                    best = cand
                cur[i] = best
            prev = cur

        return prev[n]
```
